      ├── reviewer_window.py   ← Reviewer context-window SSOT for every review surface (triad, scope, plan, deep self-review): ONE typed `ReviewerWindow` per ROUTE (window/status/stale/observed_at + computed `blocking_authority_allowed`, v6.87.44) with a metadata-only probe serialised by a per-route lock and rate-limited by `probe`'s own evidence TTL, never by a process-lifetime memo that would outlive the record (v6.87.45), fail-closed sub-floor when no evidence exists, and output/tokenizer reserves scaled to a sub-1M window so a small-window slot gets a fit-sized pack instead of a zero limit (v6.87.22; replaces the hardcoded 1M assumption each surface carried)
      ├── triad_review.py      ← Shared multi-model review primitives: JSON-array extraction is reused by repo + skill review; per-actor records, quorum/degraded accounting, and model-error events power the skill-review path. It also owns the review OUTPUT CONTRACT text rendered by the repo-triad, repo-advisory and scope prompts (skill review states its own contract in `skill_review.py`): findings-only (`REVIEW_JSON_ARRAY_CONTRACT`) and required-matrix (`REVIEW_JSON_MATRIX_CONTRACT`, no all-clear — advisory selects it whenever `expected_items` is supplied). A clean findings-only verdict is recognised only when the WHOLE response — modulo one optional code fence — is `[]`, optionally followed by the `NO_FINDINGS` sentinel and nothing else. Any surrounding prose, and the sentinel without the array, are parse failures: a refusal cannot be distinguished from a benign preamble by structure, so neither is accepted. (A valid non-empty array is of course the normal findings path; it is simply never a *clean* verdict, even with the sentinel appended.) Keeping the text beside `empty_array_is_verified_clean` — the parser that enforces it — is what stops the two from drifting apart

Exact discovery ids only (ordered per-family alias table); never pins a credential profile; validates its own output through reviewer_slot_config.parse_reviewer_slots rather than keeping a second schema

Stdlib only — no env reads, no settings file, no import of `config` (which imports THIS and re-exports the names, so `config.EFFORT_SCALE` keeps resolving). The boundary is the point: a closed enum, a rank over it, or a transform of an already-read value lives here; resolving a CONFIGURED value stays in `config.py`

      ├── task_results.py      ← Durable task result/status files (task_results/<id>.json)
      ├── task_status.py       ← Effective task-status SSOT: child-drive result merge, lineage lookup, bounded waits
      ├── git_shell_policy.py  ← Structural git argv classifiers for shell safety guards
      ├── protected_artifacts.py ← Task-contract protected artifact policy helpers for execute-only black-box references
      ├── shell_parse.py       ← Shared shell argv/inline-command parser helpers used by guardrails without importing the tools package; (v6.51.0) `recover_stringified_argv` (the SSOT JSON/AST stringified-argv recovery shared by run_command + verify_and_record) and `normalize_check_argv` (the verify check→argv SSOT that the shell guard AND execution both call, so the guard inspects exactly what runs; string → non-login `sh -c`); (v6.78.0) `shell_tokens_typed` (THE tokenizer of the module — tokens paired with whether each is real SYNTAX rather than a literal argument that spells like one, a distinction `shlex` destroys when it strips quotes; `_normalize_shell_source` marks quoted/escaped punctuation on the way in and the mark never leaves), its text view `shell_tokens` (what `shell_segments` and the guards read — a quoted `&&` still reads as a separator there, which over-splits and is the fail-safe direction) and `canonical_command_text` (the comparison-stable form of a command: one space BETWEEN tokens, token contents and control operators verbatim, nothing dropped and nothing re-classified — the seam `_outcome_receipts` derives a verification's check identity from, so neither collapsing whitespace inside a quoted argument nor stripping a literal `'&&'` argument as if it were syntax can make two different checks compare equal)
      ├── argv_budget.py       ← Byte-accurate argv/env admission — the E2BIG hygiene SSOT (C5): counts the ENCODED bytes of the argv strings AND the environment block together against POSIX ARG_MAX (with pointer/bookkeeping headroom), enforces the Linux per-string MAX_ARG_STRLEN cap (128 KiB) portably plus the Windows ~32 767-unit command-line cap, and is the single helper every subprocess-building surface (skill_exec, the benchmark CLI adapters) asks before exec — the prior char-count-only check under-counted UTF-8 by up to 4x and ignored the environment
      ├── workspace_ref.py     ← (RWS v2) The SINGLE persisted placement descriptor: sealed/immutable discriminated `WorkspaceRef` union (`local | ssh`; `docker_exec` deliberately RESERVED — docker execution stays an executor-derived projection of a local placement), serialized into task metadata (`_sealed_workspace_ref`) and queue snapshots; legacy records (bare `workspace_root`) normalize additively to the local variant on read; `isolate_sealed_placement` detaches the sealed payload from the creator's dict at queue insertion (the queue keeps a shallow copy, so an aliased payload would make an already-queued task's target retroactively editable — reads are safe on their own, since every read returns a FROZEN dataclass); `workspace_display_root` is the placement's own root spelling for the owner-visible plumbing, a string on purpose; a local ref materializes to a real `pathlib.Path`, an SSH ref has NO Home path and raises typed `RemoteWorkspacePathError` at this seam BEFORE any consumer can `.resolve()` a remote spelling. Rationale: `ExecutorRef` is a DERIVED projection of this ref, never stored independently — a placement/executor disagreement is structurally impossible because only one authority is persisted. Dependency-light (stdlib only) so transport/execd code may import the contract. Also the ROOT-PLACEMENT MATRIX (ratified Q2а): `SSH_NATIVE_ROOTS = {active_workspace}` vs `HOME_NATIVE_ROOTS` (system_repo, runtime_data, task_drive, artifact_store, user_files, subagent_projects, deliverables, skill_payload) with the `root_is_target_native(ref, root)` predicate — docker is absent by design, since docker execution is an executor-derived projection of a LOCAL placement and all of its roots stay Home paths the executor maps; plus `normalize_remote_root_relative`, the pure-`posixpath` analogue of `tool_access.normalize_root_relative` (a target path must never be normalized by Home `pathlib`: a Windows Home rewrites separators and a Home `resolve()` consults the WRONG filesystem for symlinks and existence). Rationale: a guard that calls `Path.exists()` silently asserts "the target is this Home filesystem" — for an SSH placement that assertion is both WRONG and SUCCESSFUL, which is precisely the "one policy × N doors" postmortem class; naming every fact a guard may need, once, makes the wrong door unreachable instead of merely discouraged. Interpreter provability delegates to `python_interpreter.usable_executable` (one probe, consulted through the neutral door), and the module imports NO Home policy authority so the guards that import facts are never imported back
      ├── remote_export_policy.py ← (RWS v2 §3.2) The HOME AUTHORITY over what may leave a remote host — one policy, two ends, a hash in between. It is the only place that DECIDES a policy document, because deciding reads the task contract, the resource policy and `protected_artifacts` (reused as the single source of the rules, not restated) and projects them onto TARGET-relative spellings; `policy_for_operation` is called once per operation at PREPARE and the document travels in the prepared call's `_export_policy` so its hash binds the rules that actually run. `validate_returned_manifest` then re-evaluates the SAME document over the paths that came back — hash, exported paths, the count/list arithmetic and the reason codes — and raises a typed `ExportPolicyViolation` on any disagreement. Never imported by execd: it is on the `home_policy_authority` forbidden-import list, and the DOCUMENT (`export_policy_contract`) is what travels

The after rows and the touched-path set are produced by IMPORTING the target's own `snapshot_workspace` and `_patch_numstat_paths` rather than reimplementing them, because a second implementation of a fingerprint or of a rename record is a second answer to what the tree is, and the two would refuse each other's correct patches the moment they drift.       ├── remote_plan_review.py ← (RWS v2, D12) Plan review and turn-diff EVIDENCE over a remote workspace. `open_plan_subject` materializes the verified mirror the reviewer reads (channel `workspace_snapshot` — one document per purpose) and RETURNS it: the caller owns the lifetime through the review's own `ExitStack`, which is the de-mutation of the donor's `ctx._remote_plan_review_snapshot` (a temporary directory whose lifetime was a mutable attribute that a decorator deleted and two unrelated modules read, one of them as a boolean "is this verified" flag). `plan_subject_root`, `snapshot_omission_rows`, `remote_snapshot_evidence` and `verified_snapshot_result` all take the mirror as a PARAMETER, so the omission list and the tree it describes are provably the same object — the donor's version could be asked about a snapshot already closed and deleted. `verified_snapshot_result` is shaped like the `git show HEAD:<path>` result it replaces (a materialized mirror has no git history, so a filesystem read IS its HEAD snapshot, and it is evidence because materialization verified every byte), which keeps `build_head_snapshot_section`'s size/binary/sensitive handling ONE code path for both placements. `remote_turn_diff` answers `review_evidence.collect_turn_diff` with the native `vcs_diff` operation — computed ON the target, one operation instead of a second full tree export — memoized per task with a bounded age so the several evidence consumers in one review tick ask once and a memo can never answer for an earlier turn. Rationale: before this, `collect_turn_diff`'s `active_repo_dir()` raised for a remote placement and its except arm fell back to `ctx.repo_dir`, so a remote task's review evidence was a diff of the OUROBOROS repository presented as the task's own working tree. D7: a policy-filtered mirror is REVIEWED with the omission named in the evidence horizon (a reviewer not told about an exclusion reasons as if the tree were whole), while a plan that NAMES a withheld path is refused by name — reviewing a plan about an absence is worse than refusing to
      ├── remote_task_files.py ← (RWS v2 §3.2, D12 channel `attachment_stage`) The HOME half of the task-attachment channel — the producer the closed registry declared and nothing implemented, which is why an attachment on a remote task used to be silently Home-only. WHICH SIDE holds the bytes is decided per call by the ROOT the path belongs to, because these three tools take a bare `path` with no root label and the root matrix (`workspace_ref.SSH_NATIVE_ROOTS`, Q2а) puts only `active_workspace` on the target: a remote task's artifacts, task scratch, uploads and skill state are HOME roots, and asking the target for one of them fetched the wrong file or nothing. `_media_source_on_target` asks `vision.home_file_root_for` — the SAME containment the local handler enforces, asked as a question instead of applied as a verdict, so one path classifies identically on both placements instead of two resolvers drifting — and a Home-owned path is left ALONE for the ordinary handler while a target path is folded to its workspace-relative spelling (`normalize_remote_root_relative`, the same normalization every root-labelled tool's path gets) and imported. A path inside no admitted root is refused on HOME rather than by the target: the target refuses an absolute spelling anyway, but a round trip spent learning what Home already knew answers "could not be imported from the remote workspace" about a path that was never about the remote workspace. Nothing here reads the SHAPE of the string — a prefix guess is how a Home artifact became a target lookup in the first place. `remote_media_predispatch` then rewrites the tool's path argument to the published Home artifact BEFORE any path-judging guard runs, so the guards judge the file that will actually be opened and the model sees the Home identity, never the target path (D9); the derived Home path lands inside the task's own artifact store, a root the media trust boundary already admits, so no new read surface appears. So no parallel artifact identity/lifecycle authority exists, and remote source paths never reach the public record (D9). Redaction happens BEFORE publication, so a record's hash binds the bytes actually in it rather than the source digest of content nobody stored. Home decides its own bounds (model preview, trace keys, artifact count) because a remote envelope must not be able to set the size of a Home record. The cut follows a seam rather than a line count: the broker owns LIFECYCLE (request queue, worker channels, panic, leases) while this answers one narrower question — may this placement use a session, and which one. FUNCTIONS over a broker, not methods on it, so the coupling is visible in every signature; the broker keeps three thin delegating methods because they are what its dispatch table names
      ├── remote_workspace.py ← (RWS v2 §3.1) The server-generation owner of EVERY SSH session: exactly ONE `RemoteSessionBroker` per generation, created in `server.py` lifespan BEFORE worker recovery and closed FIRST at teardown. Workers never build transports — they get one Pipe endpoint each. Three properties are load-bearing and each has a named test in `tests/test_remote_broker_lifecycle.py`: (1) **proxy lifecycle is bound to worker generation** — channels are keyed by OWNER (`worker:3`) in `remote_worker_proxy.WorkerChannels`, so `respawn_worker` REPLACES a dead worker's endpoint instead of leaving a second live channel the poll loop would answer into, `kill_workers` closes all, and every proxy call carries the generation it was minted for so a stale call gets typed `BROKER_GENERATION_STALE` (refused, never hung); (2) **panic never waits** — it snapshots the `_panic_transports`/`_panic_events` custody registers, takes `_state_lock` only with `blocking=False`, and calls each transport's own non-waiting panic, because a panic that could block on the ordinary broker lock would be a software delay in the one path forbidden to have one. Panic clears both registers AFTER its loops, never before. `SSHBrowserForwardManager._panic_processes` was the same defect with the same fix, pruned inside `_terminate`/`_terminate_many` — the functions that end the process. Both Home seams are INJECTED at construction (`home_importer` handed to every transport, `pending_recovery` for durable reconciliation); with no recovery hook, unreconciled scopes are REPORTED as `reconcile_unavailable` rather than returning an empty list that would read as "all clean"

      ├── remote_worker_proxy.py ← (RWS v2 §3.1) Both ends of the worker channel in one module: `RemoteWorkspacePipeProxy` (the worker side — one Pipe endpoint, never an SSH handle; stamped with the server generation; every failure typed and deadline-bounded: closed pipe, protocol mismatch, timeout, stale generation) and `WorkerChannels` (the broker side — owner-keyed endpoints so minting for the same worker id RETIRES its previous channel, plus the send locks and the dead-endpoint drop the poll loop needs). Its `ssh -G` check is deliberately its own and stricter in a different direction than `remote_ssh_config`'s: it requires that the recorded `-L` be the ONLY forward, and rejects any RemoteForward/DynamicForward/SetEnv/Tunnel/RemoteCommand/LocalCommand effect plus an owner `ControlMaster`/`ControlPersist` (no owner control socket is reused or left alive).       ├── remote_ssh.py        ← (RWS v2 §5, transport) SESSION TRANSPORT ONLY: one multiplexed framed execd session over a supervised OpenSSH child. Owns the child, the strict nonce-preamble scan, the reader/stderr/lease threads, prepare→continue/abort→result bound to the prepared token, bounded blob upload/download with per-chunk ACKs, reconnect/reconcile, cancel, and a panic that best-effort writes ONE non-blocking panic frame then tears its children down WITHOUT awaiting an ACK (the remote custodian owns the unreachable case). Teardown releases a child's pipes through `_release_child_streams`, which closes the RAW stream — the object that OWNS the descriptor. OPEN-6 (PR 79) was the alternative: `os.close(stream.fileno())` closes the descriptor behind its owner's back, the owner still believes it is open, the OS reuses the descriptor NUMBER for the next child, and finalizing the stale owner then closes a LIVE process's pipe — after which closing that process's own `stdout` raises `OSError: [Errno 9] Bad file descriptor`, exactly the CI symptom of an SSH probe issued after `broker.panic()`. Closing the raw stream also does NOT flush the buffered wrapper, which is the property that made `os.close` tempting: a forked child must never push bytes into the parent's SSH pipe, and panic must never wait on a flush. A lease ANSWER is consumed by the reader thread (`_observe_lease_answer`), never by a waiter: `_renew_lease` stays a bare write because the lease path also carries panic and cancel and may never acquire a wait, and one unclaimed frame per renewal would otherwise fill the bounded pending queue and kill a healthy session. A refused lease is kept in one slot and disclosed through `health()` rather than raised — it arrives on no caller's thread, and it means this Home's generation no longer owns the remote process groups, so its remote work is already condemned to the lease ceiling. Control traffic and bulk traffic take SEPARATE locks, so lease renewal/cancel/panic stay live while a blob upload is backpressured. The OpenSSH child is spawned through `process_custody.spawn_supervised(scope="session", required_custody=True)` — a child the reaper cannot see would survive panic, so a failed ledger write kills it instead of returning it. Rationale (the postmortem's dominant class): this module imports NO Home policy authority — not `artifacts`, not `observability`, not `remote_transfer`, not `tool_access` — and its ONLY Home interaction is the `HomeImporter` protocol injected at construction (`self.home_importer`), reached solely through `remote_reconciliation`. It is therefore a SEED of the §3.3 import-closure gate while deliberately NOT being a bundle module: gated in the reverse direction, never shipped to the target
      ├── remote_ssh_config.py ← (RWS v2 §5; mandated pre-split of `remote_ssh.py`, which was AT the 1600-line gate BEFORE adaptation) Stateless validation of what OpenSSH ACTUALLY resolves for an alias. Ouroboros never parses `~/.ssh/config` — system OpenSSH owns authentication, so Host blocks/ProxyJump/IdentityFile/agent/strict `known_hosts` apply untouched. Instead the final argv is CONSTRUCTED first (fixed `-o` overrides: `BatchMode=yes`, `RemoteCommand=none`, `RequestTTY=no`, `ForwardAgent=no`, `ForwardX11=no`, `PermitLocalCommand=no`, `ClearAllForwardings=yes`, `Tunnel=no`, `ControlMaster/Path/Persist=no`, operational timeouts from its OWN `get_ssh_timeout_sec` — the env-only table lives here, in the module that builds the options, because reaching `ouroboros.config` for it put a `settings_or_owner_state` dependency on the transport that the §3.3 reverse gate forbids; `config.get_ssh_timeout_sec` delegates here) and then resolved through `ssh -G` TWICE — once WITH those overrides (does the channel survive this alias?) and once RAW (what did the owner actually ask for?). Checking only the overridden view hides a hostile alias; checking only the raw view rejects aliases the overrides already neutralize. Refused before spawn: a surviving `RemoteCommand` or forced TTY (would corrupt/block frames), any effective `SetEnv`, a `SendEnv` pattern matching a key retained in the constructed child environment (`SSH_ENV_KEYS`; `SendEnv=-*`/`SetEnv=-*` are deliberately NOT used — Phase 0 proved they do not generically cancel Host-block values), and forwarding/tunnel directives that survive the overrides. Neutralized-but-present forwarding is DISCLOSED as a warning rather than silently dropped. Raw `ssh -G` output, SetEnv values and IdentityFile paths stay memory-only; durable diagnostics carry bounded names/reasons only. Also owns `transport_error`/`safe_text`/`minimal_ssh_env` — the child environment is constructed from retained keys, never inherited from Home
      ├── remote_ssh_bootstrap.py ← (RWS v2 §4.5, transport core; donor as-is apart from the error seam) Immutable execd bundle selection and ATOMIC remote installation: architecture/libc probe → manifest+SHA-256 verified locally → upload to a validated temporary path → verify remotely → extract into a fresh mode-0700 directory under the manifest allowlist (absolute/parent-traversing names, duplicate destinations, devices/FIFOs, escaping links and entry-count/expanded-byte overflow all rejected BEFORE writing) → verify the expanded tree → atomically switch the `current` pointer. Idempotent and concurrency-safe: an interrupted, bad-hash or wrong-architecture attempt removes only its own temporary directory and leaves the previous version and pointer usable
      ├── remote_service_leases.py ← (RWS v2 §5.4, transport core; donor as-is) The broker's bounded book of remote service leases: normalized nonsecret service metadata, per-connection/per-session-key liveness, and the candidate set that panic/cancel/connection-retire operate on. A service ID resolves its stored placement, so status/log/stop act on the backend where the service actually started
      ├── remote_pending_operations.py ← (RWS v2 §3.2, transport half of the donor's `remote_pending_operations` split) Durable journal of in-flight remote mutations: one fsynced typed intent per mutation written BEFORE Home may send CONTINUE (`write_pending_operation` → `atomic_write_json(fsync=True, mode=0o600, fsync_directory=True)`), its reload into transport tracking after a restart (`restore_transport_tracking`), scope grouping for reconciliation (`pending_operation_groups`), the intent→operation binding (`bind_transport_intent` — a mutation with no closed durable import kind cannot proceed at all), the pre-reconciliation identity gate (`validate_transport_session_identity`: changed host/workspace/canonical-root/capability identity is a typed refusal demanding owner re-trust, never an import of somebody else's bytes) and the fsynced removal of a satisfied intent. Records carry ONLY identities, the prepared hash and the closed import contract — no prepared token, no canonical argv, no blob bytes, no connection secret: the journal is evidence, not a replay buffer. The Home RECOVERY half (`resolve_pending_recovery_group`/`recover_pending_on_broker`, which need `gateway.connections` + `projects_registry` + the broker) deliberately does NOT live here — that is the split's whole point
      ├── remote_reconciliation.py ← (RWS v2 §3.2, transport half of the donor's `remote_finalization` split) Verification of what a remote result CLAIMS, and reconciliation of a reopened session's operation ledger. `prefetch_remote_result_import` fetches a blob only when a declaration names it, bounds every fetch before allocation (per-stream 16 MiB, declared outputs 32 MiB aggregate, one whole-import ceiling) and accepts bytes only against their own size+SHA-256; the externalized envelope is parsed strictly (duplicate keys, floats, oversized integers, depth and item counts all rejected) and a wire output-projection that contradicts the envelope's artifact refs fails closed. `reconcile_remote_operations` is not a retry engine: `completed` imports then ACKs, `completed` + unavailable stored result becomes durable terminal evidence (retention pruning that can never touch a live `*.pending.json` intent) and is never permission to repeat the mutation, and only a proven `not_started` drops the intent. Rationale: the Home import itself happens behind the INJECTED `HomeImporter.complete_import` seam (`transport.home_importer`), so the module that verifies remote bytes imports no Home authority — the exact inverted dependency that dominated the PR 79 postmortem
      ├── remote_contracts.py  ← (RWS v2 §3.2) The REGISTER of every Home↔execd contract and `CONTRACT_SET_VERSION`, the single carrier of "may these two builds cooperate". Stdlib only and imports neither half, so both may cite it — the same property that lets `export_policy_contract` travel. It holds the register itself (`CONTRACTS`: wire, native operations, capability manifest, export policy, import channels, prepared call, attachment staging, execd state schemas, lease, execution envelope, reconciliation), the two owner ACTIONS (`bootstrap_connection`, `rebuild_execd_bundle` — not interchangeable: one re-installs the target from the bundle this Home ships, the other says the bundle IS the stale artifact), `ContractDriftError` + `refuse_unknown_members` (the ONE typed refusal every contract answers an unrecognized member with, naming which contract, which members, what this build understands and what to do; a `ValueError` subclass so every `except ValueError` boundary is unchanged), `contract_set_compatible` (EQUALITY, the cooperation question — distinct from `remote_protocol.protocol_compatible`, the looser parsing one over the same number) and `contract_skew_refusal` (one `(code, message, details)` for all four admission seams, perspective-neutral because two of them run on Home and one on the target). Rationale for reusing `PROTOCOL_MINOR` as the carrier rather than adding a field: the number then already travels in the preamble and both handshake frames of every build ever shipped, which is the only reason a target installed BEFORE this check can be refused at all
      ├── remote_contract_admission.py ← (RWS v2 §3.2) The two seams that ASK, one per side, kept together because they are one decision taken twice and a reader checking "is a mismatched pair refused before it can do damage?" needs both answers in one place rather than one buried in the transport and one in the executor. `admit_home_contract_set` raises the ordinary bootstrap-phase `transport_error` so the refusal travels the route Connections, the CLI and the task result already read; `admit_execd_contract_set` answers on STDERR and then ends the session, because a handshake carries no `request_id` for a diagnostic to use and no control kind an older Home would recognize exists — stderr is the one channel every Home build already reads (`details.stderr` of the session error)
      ├── remote_refusal_actions.py ← (RWS v2) THE register of WHICH OWNER ACTION REMOVES a remote refusal, and nothing else. `REFUSAL_ACTIONS` maps every refusal code this feature can emit to the single action that clears it, and `workspace_diagnostics.RemoteWorkspaceError.__init__` reads it — so ~40 raise sites gained a correct action without one of them being edited, and a code absent from the table is a test failure rather than a silent `retry`. The defect it answers is a refusal with the WRONG action, which is worse than one with none: the owner presses what they were told to press, it succeeds, and the block is still there, having taught them that the system's advice is noise (live instance: an unselectable connection was told to run Test, `Test` returned `ok` with `health_fresh: true`, and the block stayed, because only Bootstrap writes the contract-set stamp). `connection_blocker` is the ordered ladder for the other direction — why can this connection not carry a project, and what removes THAT — and both browser surfaces plus the CLI render it rather than deriving their own. The table is cross-checked against an INDEPENDENT authority that already existed (`cli_connections._UNSERVABLE_CODES`, the set the owner CLI maps to exit 4), so a code that cannot be cured by retrying may not advise one; proofs in `tests/test_remote_refusal_action_proofs.py`
      ├── remote_protocol.py   ← (RWS v2, transport core) Dependency-light (stdlib-only) wire protocol for the Home↔execd OpenSSH stdio channel: canonical JSON encoding, length-prefixed UTF-8 control frames + separate bounded binary bulk frames, per-session nonce preamble scanned within a fixed byte budget (framing never resynchronizes through arbitrary bytes after it), frame/string/depth limits checked BEFORE allocation, monotonically scoped sequence numbers, order-independent `canonical_prepared_hash`, minor-version compatibility window (the minor IS `remote_contracts.CONTRACT_SET_VERSION`, and this window answers the PARSING question only — session admission requires equality), and the independent control reader (`run_control_reader`) that keeps lease renewal/cancel/panic flowing while bulk traffic is backpressured (at most one unacknowledged bulk frame in flight). Lease renewal is ANSWERED — `lease_answer_marker`/`lease_answer_id` define the `optional` marker that makes an ordinary `ack` (honored, echoing the accepted `lease_id` + `server_generation`) or `diagnostic` (refused) a LEASE answer. It lives here because both halves must agree on it and neither may import the other, and it reuses the two EXISTING shapes instead of adding a control kind: an unknown kind fails the session by contract, while `optional` is the declared extension slot, so a peer that never reads the marker is unaffected by its presence and no protocol version moves. Rationale for answering at all: an accepted lease and a dropped frame are indistinguishable on a silent wire, so the constitutional bound the lease carries (§3.4/BIBLE: the custodian ends remote work no later than 15s after the last renewal it accepted) had NO representation in the protocol and was unobservable to Home, to the acceptance ledger and to any audit — an invariant that cannot be observed cannot be proven. Rationale: JSONL alone cannot survive child/MOTD output and binary transfer; strict framing after a bounded preamble makes a corrupted stream fail the session instead of desynchronizing it
      ├── workspace_diagnostics.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) Dependency-neutral owner of the structured execution envelope shared by local and execd contexts: `ExecutionDiagnostic` (domain/code/phase/completion/retryable/errno), `ProcessExecutionResult`, compositional `ToolExecutionEnvelope`, errno→stable-code mapping (`diagnostic_from_exception` — never parses rendered English strings), and the text renderer both sides share. The donor's request-scoped contextvar publication (`publish_execution_envelope`/`current_execution_envelope`/`reset_execution_envelope`) and its `envelope_from_exception`/`process_execution_envelope` helpers were DELETED at synthesis: §3.1 replaced ambient per-request state with an operation-scoped context that travels with the operation object (codex #16), so an envelope published into a contextvar had no reader left — and keeping the mechanism would have preserved the very ambient channel the operation-scoped context exists to remove; every dataclass self-sanitizes secrets on construction (`sanitize_execution_text`). (RWS v2 §3.2) Also the home of `RemoteWorkspaceError` — the typed nonsecret remote-placement error that projects itself into an `ExecutionDiagnostic`; it lives beside the diagnostic rather than in the broker so the journal, the transport, the broker and the worker proxy can each raise it without importing a layer above themselves (the donor reached it through a function-local `remote_workspace` import from inside the transport). Rationale: one envelope authority means stdout/stderr/returncode have no second source of truth on either placement
      ├── export_policy_contract.py ← (RWS v2 §3.2) The export-policy DOCUMENT and its ONE mechanical evaluator, shared by BOTH sides of the remote boundary: the rule tables (excluded dirs, credential-like names/prefixes/suffixes, sensitive path components, credential markers, deliverable component names), the closed `EXPORT_CHANNELS` blob-kind registry (Appendix C-1; unknown kind fails closed via `channel_profile`), `normalize_export_policy` (fail-closed on unknown fields/version/channel — as a TYPED `ContractDriftError` naming the contract and the owner's action, because the bare `ValueError: export policy has unknown fields: [...]` this used to raise is exactly what a build pair's disagreement looked like to the owner), `canonical_policy_bytes`/`export_policy_hash` (the hash that rides with the prepared operation so source and Home compare ONE policy), the private ladder `_exclusion_reason` (the single predicate, and the ONLY function that reads a rule group — the deliverable component rule is private behind it, because a door that asked that group DIRECTLY was applying one rule out of a document carrying two; the ladder itself is private because eight call sites reached it and skipped the mandatory `question`), its stricter `deliverable` profile, and EXACTLY TWO public ways to judge a path: `judged_exclusion` — THE target-side judge, which answers over the requested spelling, the RESOLVED identity (`identity_spelling`) and the RECURSIVE hardlink alias (`AliasIndex`) under one of the closed `EXPORT_QUESTIONS` (`export` / `named_source` / `mutation` / `none`, a question being which rule groups THIS door may act on) — and `unaliased_exclusion`, the SPELLING-only door, named for what it cannot do and reserved for Home, which holds no workspace and can only re-evaluate the strings a manifest returned. `refuse_excluded_target` is `judged_exclusion` plus the raise, for doors that refuse rather than disclose. `AliasIndex` is the per-operation IDENTITY CONTEXT: the canonical root, a per-directory `realpath` memo, a per-directory excluded-inode listing and the lazily-built whole-tree index, so the alias question costs one `stat` for a single-named file (the ordinary case, short-circuited on `st_nlink < 2`), two directory listings for the shapes that actually occur, and one traversal only for a genuinely distant alias, plus `export_disclosure_block` (the additive D7 block: `complete`/`integrity_complete`/`policy_scope`/exact `excluded_count`/bounded `excluded[]` AND the symmetric `exported[]`, which is what gives Home's returned-manifest check something to re-evaluate) and `MANIFEST_EXPORTED_PATH_FIELDS`, the declaration Home derives that check's field list from. Rationale: the rules travel as DATA because the same predicate written twice drifts — five copies of these tables existed before this module (workspace_snapshot_native inline ×3, workspace_query_native._sensitive_patch_path, workspace_payload_native, tools/shell.py), which is the postmortem's "one policy × N doors" class. Stdlib plus the equally dependency-free contract register (`remote_contracts`, which names this document as one of the contracts a build pair must share), so the execd bundle imports it; the Home AUTHORITY that DECIDES a document (`remote_export_policy`) is on the forbidden-import list and never travels
      ├── workspace_native_contract.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) Public contract of the execd native workspace kernel: the closed `MANDATORY_REMOTE_NATIVE_OPERATIONS` set + operation→module map (`validate_remote_native_operation_map` fails on missing/unexpected entries), `REMOTE_NATIVE_KERNEL_MODULES` bundle-closure list, `NativePreparedOperation`/`NativeOperationResult` shapes, the BUNDLED authorize-phase fact block both sides agree on (`NATIVE_FACT_PATH_STATS`/`_GIT_TOPLEVELS`/`_INTERPRETERS` wire names + `target_path_stat`/`bundle_target_facts`/`bundle_prepared_facts`, which derive Home's `PathFact`/`GitFact`/`InterpreterFact` shapes from the resolutions prepare already performed — filled here, read by `execution_facts.RemoteExecutionFacts`, and capped as a REFUSAL rather than a truncation because a silently short block would let a guard authorize over facts it only thinks it has), `NativeExecutionControl` custody protocol, `BoundedProcessStream` (hash-whole/preview-head+tail process capture), the `ProcessSpool`/`ProcessSpoolSink` protocols the kernel drives an execd-owned log spool through (plus `open_process_spool_sinks`/`seal_process_spool_sinks`/`process_log_artifact`/`process_capture_trace`/`process_quota_notes` — one place decides whether a stream's full-log artifact is a sealed remote blob, an inline blob, or honestly absent), and the process-text renderers shared byte-identically by Home and target (`render_process_result_text`, `describe_process_returncode`, `format_process_output`, grep autocorrect/no-match classifiers). `SERVICE_NAME_PATTERN` also lives here rather than in `tools/services`, because the TARGET is the side that turns a service name into a log filename and a second copy is how the two routes came to disagree (the local one refused, the target's `re.sub` silently rewrote). `native_relative_spelling` refuses NUL and control characters as well as absolute paths and traversals — parity with `utils.safe_relpath`, matched exactly (`\t\n\r` excluded on both sides), since a native route refusing MORE would be a new asymmetry pointing the other way. Depends only on stdlib + workspace_diagnostics so the execd bundle can import it without Home code
      ├── workspace_edit_native.py ← Target-native `apply_patch` and `edit_batch`: pure applications of `patch_core` through the target's write door, no process, no git, no transport. Every mutation path is derived from `native_mutation_target` AT the mutation rather than from a map filled earlier, so a destructive act cannot reach a path no reader can trace back to the policy that admitted it
      ├── workspace_native.py  ← (RWS v2, transport core) Target-native dispatch of the closed native-operation set (read/write/edit/list/search/run_command/run_script/services/vcs/query/snapshot/payload): every path fact, symlink check, cwd resolve, interpreter resolve and process spawn happens ON the target filesystem with real target semantics — never a Home `Path` over a remote spelling; foreground/service processes run in their own process groups under `NativeExecutionControl` custody (register→run→release on one ownership path across normal return/timeout/cancel), stdout/stderr drain through `BoundedProcessStream` into bounded previews + whole-stream hashes AND, when the control object carries an operation-bound `process_spool` (execd does), through incremental spool writes whose sealed blob id lands in the result trace — so an oversized stream yields a fetchable full log instead of the donor's silent "no artifact past 16 MiB". Shared by execd (remote placement) and the local native backend so semantics are placement-blind by construction. Every path it opens comes from `workspace_native_paths` (the confinement kernel, extracted at the module-size gate rather than grandfathered) — including `.ouroboros/services/<name>.log` and the staged script directory, which live INSIDE the workspace and so are model-redirectable, and which were assembled with `root / ...` until a symlink there was shown to escape. `start_service` now REFUSES a name the local route refuses instead of `re.sub`-ing it into a filename (two names collapsed to one log, so `service_logs` could return another service's output) and refuses a NEGATIVE readiness timeout instead of clamping it to zero, which looked exactly like a service that came up instantly; the name rule itself is `workspace_native_contract.SERVICE_NAME_PATTERN`, imported by `tools/services` rather than restated there
      ├── workspace_native_paths.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) The target's PATH-CONFINEMENT KERNEL, and the reason it is its own module: TWO doors and no third. `native_target` resolves a READ spelling whole (final component included) and refuses anything landing outside the root; `native_mutation_target` is the ONE door every native mutation walks through — open, append, atomic replace, snapshot restore, service log, staged script — confining the parent by resolution AND resolving the final component when it is a symlink, refusing an out-of-root link and FOLLOWING an in-root one, which is exactly what the local route's `tool_access.resolve_resource_path` does in both directions. It used to confine only the parent, which was correct for the single caller it was written against (`atomic_write`, where `os.replace` substitutes the link and never follows it) and wrong as a RULE: `write_file` with `mode="append"` opened the same path with `"a"`, followed a workspace symlink, and wrote outside the workspace on the target (reproduced live). The lesson is the module boundary, not the patch — a confinement decided at the open site is a decision the next open site can make differently, so the rule lives here and `tests/test_target_confinement_and_disclosure` FAILS when a native mutation is added that does not derive its path from this door. An unanswerable `is_symlink()` refuses (fail-closed): a mutation proceeding on an unanswered confinement question is the same defect with a different first step. Travels in the execd bundle (`REMOTE_NATIVE_KERNEL_MODULES`) because the target is where a path is actually opened, so Home's mirror of the rule is not where the refusal can happen
      ├── workspace_media_native.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) The media EXPORT channel and nothing else: ffmpeg resolution with the pinned-digest check execd requires, and frame extraction under `NativeExecutionControl` custody. Split out of `workspace_native.py` at the module-size gate rather than grandfathered (plan §5). Takes an ALREADY-resolved, ALREADY-policy-checked source path: the export policy is applied at PREPARE, before ffmpeg is resolved and before a subprocess exists, so no byte of an excluded file is ever read
      ├── workspace_query_native.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) Placement-shared query semantics: `search_code`/`query_code`/`list_files` classification and git workspace operations (`vcs_status`/`vcs_diff`) built on target-native process/FS facts; search returns `complete`/`partial` with bounded skipped paths and reasons — an unreadable candidate can never produce an authoritative empty claim (identical local/SSH behavior is the point of the module). Every byte-bearing query channel runs the export policy at the source: the search walk, the `vcs_status`/`vcs_diff` pathspec (built by evaluating THE policy over `git ls-files` rather than re-expressing the rules as git globs — a second dialect of one policy is how doors drift), and `export_workspace_patch`, which now EXCLUDES the omitted paths from the diff and discloses the omission instead of refusing the whole export the way the donor did (a single `.env` took plan review and every other snapshot consumer down with it). **Filtering is only half the contract: every query exclusion is also DISCLOSED** through `_PolicyExclusions` → the same `export_disclosure_block` the declared-output channel emits, so `search_code`/`query_code` return `completion=partial`, `policy_scope=policy_filtered`, an exact `excluded_count` and a bounded `excluded[]`, and the owner-facing text names the count and the paths. Rationale: silent filtering did not leak bytes, it manufactured a FALSE CONCLUSION — `search_code("SECRET_TOKEN")` answered "No matches found … (2 files searched)" with `completion=complete` over a workspace whose `.env` held that string, while the same query on a LOCAL workspace returned the line (local `_code_search` has no export policy and `.env` is not in its skip globs), so the model reasoned "the key is not here" from a false premise — D7 partial-disclosure and §9 cross-placement parity failing together. `excluded_directory` (`.git`, `__pycache__`, …) is deliberately NOT disclosed because both placements prune those silently; disclosing them would flip every ordinary query to `partial` and bury the sensitive/protected class that actually diverges
      ├── workspace_snapshot_native.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) Content-addressed workspace snapshot/patch primitive: stable before/after manifest snapshot under bounded conflict retry, blob hashing, and `guarded_patch_apply` (git apply --check → default git apply; never index/3way/reject semantics), with honest `rollback_failed` reporting on hostile external races. Filtering is SOURCE-SIDE and mechanical: the walk asks `export_policy_contract.judged_exclusion` for every entry — the same call every single-source door makes and the excluded bytes are never read, because filtering after a fetch is already the leak. A policy exclusion is DISCLOSED, not fatal — `complete=false` + `integrity_complete=true` + `policy_scope=policy_filtered` + the applied `policy_hash` (D7); IO failures and an unstable tree stay fail-closed, and the two are deliberately distinguishable because conflating them turns a policy decision into an outage. The rollback loop (`_restore_rows`) resolves through `native_mutation_target` like every other native mutation: it was the last one outside the confinement kernel, protected only by a LEXICAL `..` check, and lexically clean is not confined — a rollback runs precisely when something has already gone wrong
      ├── workspace_payload_native.py ← (RWS v2, transport core) Native payload channels: inline `run_script` bodies (hash/interpreter/cwd bound in the prepared call, mode-0600 temp file removed on every exit path), reviewed skill payloads (staged blobs re-verified against the review manifest before execution), declared-output collection with per-file/total byte caps and disclosed failure notes, and scratch fingerprinting. Declared outputs are judged by the `deliverable` profile of the ONE export policy, and a credential-shaped MEMBER is excluded (disclosed, with an exact count) rather than failing the whole output — one stray `secrets/` file inside a deliverable directory used to cost the owner the entire artifact. The member's bytes are never read, so nothing excluded is in the blob and nothing has to be filtered again on Home
      ├── execd_task_files.py  ← (RWS v2, transport core) Execd-side task-file contract extracted from the donor's `remote_task_files.py`: canonical attachment manifest validation, exact content-addressed blob-set verification (`attachment_blob_map`), typed `RemoteTaskFileError`, and the execd-owned mode-0700/0600 `RemoteTaskFileCache` (atomic all-or-nothing task attachment publication, exact-manifest export, stale-generation pruning). Rationale: execd needs only the attachment CONTRACT + its private cache — the Home admission/import half (staging RPCs, envelope validation) imports the Home broker and stays OUT of the execd import closure instead of dragging a 749-line Home module into the bundle
      ├── execd_state.py       ← (RWS v2, transport core) Durable execd state authority: fail-closed `OperationJournal` (exclusive lock → write → fsync → rename → dir fsync BEFORE any handler starts; duplicate completed ops replay the stored result, same-ID/different-hash is a protocol conflict, `started` without provable result is `completion_unknown` — never an automatic repeat), mode-0600 `CASBlobStore` with pin/GC that never reclaims unacknowledged references, `LeaseCustody` + independent custodian (process groups die ≤15s after the last Home lease renewal measured on the target's own BOOT-ANCHORED monotonic clock via `platform_layer.boot_anchored_monotonic_ms` — a wall-clock step on the target may not move a constitutional bound, and the `(boot id, boottime)` anchor persisted beside the deadlines is what lets the independent custodian process date them, with an anchor mismatch reading fail-closed as EXPIRED rather than as infinite; generations are mutually non-adoptable so two Homes on one host cannot kill each other's groups), and the version-independent continuity `host_id` (missing/corrupt state on a pinned connection fails closed)
      ├── execd_spool.py       ← (RWS v2, transport core; D8 pre-split of `execd.py`) Process-log spool state machine: a PURE `(state, event) -> (state, effects)` reducer (`apply_spool_event`) for `open → sealing | terminating_on_quota → sealed → acknowledged | expired` plus the terminal failures `disk_full`/`hash_failed`/`state_corrupt`, and a thin I/O half (`ProcessLogSpool`/`SpoolStreamSink` append-only mode-0600 writes, one sealed content-addressed blob per stream, `SpoolQuotaLedger` per-stream 512 MiB / per-task 2 GiB / host-wide 8 GiB reservations taken under the SAME `execd_state.state_file_lock` the journal and custodian use, so concurrent stdout/stderr writers cannot oversubscribe the host between a read and a write). Rationale (BIBLE P1 — the execution trace is not lost): reaching a quota TERMINATES the process group and only then seals, so every accepted byte survives, instead of the donor's `BoundedProcessStream.append` dropping the whole captured stream past 16 MiB. An illegal (state, event) pair is never guessed — it becomes `state_corrupt` naming the exact pair. Reservation-based accounting (not post-hoc) is what makes the host bound safe under concurrency; a partly-used grant is conservative, never oversubscribed. RETENTION is the half that was declared and missing: `acknowledge`/`expire` live on the per-operation STREAM SINK, which dies with the operation, so a sealed log's reservation was never handed back and the host-wide 8 GiB was a one-way ratchet — it fills exactly once and then every later remote process on that host refuses to spool, with no single event to blame. The durable unit is therefore the TASK: `retention.json` (schema 1, same state-lock discipline) records each sealed blob's size, seal time and OWNER SET, written at the one moment the blob id and the owning task are both in hand. `ProcessLogSpool.release_task` runs at the task terminal (`ExecdService.cancel`) and frees the quota and the blobs; `expire_retained` runs on the custody tick (`renew_lease`) as the age backstop for the Home that died mid-task and never sent a cancel. The owner set is not decoration: sealed blobs are CONTENT-addressed, so two tasks whose stdout was byte-identical share one file and a directory-walk deletion would take the other task's evidence with it. Ordering inside `release_task` is deliberate — blobs are unlinked BEFORE the quota row is dropped, so a crash between them leaves the host looking fuller than it is (the next sweep corrects it) rather than quota freed for bytes still on disk. A corrupt index is repaired to empty instead of raising: it holds no evidence of its own, and a spool that refuses to free anything because its bookkeeping is unreadable is precisely the failure retention exists to prevent. Materializing a sealed blob on the model's demand stays DEFERRED by owner decision (`read_sealed` still has no Home producer, recorded in `tests/test_seam_producers.py`); the quota consequence of that deferral is what is closed
      ├── execd.py             ← (RWS v2, transport core) Restricted remote entrypoint over OpenSSH stdio: nonce-preamble handshake with exact release/capability attestation, fixed operation allowlist (`MANDATORY_REMOTE_NATIVE_OPERATIONS` + internal task-file ops), prepare→continue/abort→execute with immutable prepared-token binding revalidated before effect, protocol-only stdout (child output travels as events; diagnostics go to SSH stderr), oversized results externalized to the CAS spool before the result frame, every `lease` frame answered by `_answer_lease` (ack when the frame names the generation this execd owns, typed `lease_generation_mismatch` diagnostic when it names a foreign or stale one — distinct from `generation_closing`, which means the SAME generation on its way out), and panic/EOF closing every owned process group without waiting for ACK. The lease answer is emitted HERE rather than by the generic handler because a lease frame carries no `request_id`: the generic path could not address a diagnostic to it, so a refusal raised there ended the SESSION instead of refusing the one lease. It costs no extra I/O — acceptance rides the renewal custody already performs and a foreign generation is refused before the state file is touched, which is also what keeps the frequent renewal path cheap and the refusal unable to perturb the generation it is aimed at. Carries no model client, no Home policy, no Home credentials (one brain — Home keeps all authority)
      ├── tool_capabilities.py ← SSOT for tool sets (core, parallel-safe, truncation, browser); (RWS v2 §3.1) the Home/execd CAPABILITY CONTRACT — `WORKSPACE_TOOL_EXECUTION_AFFINITY` declares, per visible workspace tool, whose facts the operation is about (root/cwd/workspace/service/hybrid/home) and `HOME_ONLY_TOOL_NAMES` declares the built-ins that are deliberately NOT a workspace surface, so exhaustiveness is checked against the REGISTRY: a new built-in must be given an affinity or named Home's, and doing neither fails the build with its name. The previous claim rested on `set(WORKSPACE_TOOL_EXECUTION_AFFINITY) == set(_WORKSPACE_ALLOWED_TOOLS)` plus a `builtin_execution_affinity` accessor with zero callers outside its own test — comparing two hand-written constants proves only that they agree with each other, and a genuinely new tool in NEITHER passed every gate and was classified Home-only by silence (verified on a copy of the repo). The dead accessor is gone. **There is no skill-manifest `execution_affinity`**: marrying skills to remote placement is a DEFERRED phase (owner decision), `scripts[].execution_affinity`/`tool_execution_affinity` are read by no loader, and the docs that described them as validated and load-blocking were corrected — a placement the manifest asserts and the runtime ignores is a false safety claim; `build_workspace_capability_manifest` seals the registry's UNFILTERED public schemas + the native operation allowlist + the declared bundle kernel into a hash-bound manifest, and `assert_workspace_capability_compatible` refuses a target whose half differs, naming WHICH half. The manifest is a property of the BUILD, never of a task — filtering it per task would make two tasks on one server disagree about which targets are admissible. `native_kernel_modules` publishes `REMOTE_NATIVE_KERNEL_MODULES`, NOT the closure's roots, because the closure also seeds the Home-side transport (which never travels in the bundle). (RWS v2 §3.3) also the STRUCTURAL isolation gate for the execd bundle: `remote_native_import_closure` walks the deterministic import closure from `REMOTE_NATIVE_CLOSURE_SEEDS` over EVERY import scope (execd, execd_state, execd_spool, execd_task_files, the whole workspace_native family, remote_protocol, workspace_diagnostics, workspace_native_contract) recording the edge that pulled each module in, and `assert_remote_native_import_closure` fails on a missing seed, on a `REMOTE_NATIVE_KERNEL_MODULES` entry the closure never reaches (a stale/renamed declaration), or on any `FORBIDDEN_REMOTE_IMPORT_PREFIXES` hit — reporting the module, the concrete `a -> b` edge, and the whole chain back to its seed. The prefix table runs BOTH directions: forward (registry/provider/review/server/gateway/settings/Home task+artifact state) and the reverse `home_policy_authority` gate (`tool_access`, `tool_policy`, `protected_artifacts`, `observability`, `safety`, `workspace_admission`, `workspace_executor`, `remote_export_policy`, `remote_transfer`). The seed set holds TWO distinct classes and the distinction is load-bearing: bundle modules (execd + the workspace_native family, which travel to the target and are declared in `REMOTE_NATIVE_KERNEL_MODULES`), and the Home-side transport `remote_ssh`/`remote_ssh_config`, seeded purely for the REVERSE direction — they must not reach a Home policy authority, but they never ship in the bundle. Conflating the two would either send the transport to the target or stop gating it. Rationale: the postmortem's failure class was an execd module that reached Home by accident and only failed on the remote host, and a policy import inside the bundle is a SECOND authority by construction (one brain — Home decides, execd executes). FUNCTION-LOCAL imports are inside the boundary too, but judged differently: module-scope edges are TRAVERSED transitively (importing a module executes them — the postmortem's "arrives merely by importing" class), while a function-local import is checked as a DIRECT edge of the module that spells it and is NOT traversed (`scope=function_local` in the finding). Rationale for the asymmetry: a late import is this codebase's ordinary cycle-breaker, so following those transitively reaches 226 modules — effectively the whole program — and a gate that always fails teaches nothing; what matters is a bundle module NAMING a Home authority. This closed a real hole: the gate read module scope only and this file's comment justified it by "a clean-subprocess invocation smoke covers those per native operation", a control that DID NOT EXIST — and two violations were living in the gap (`remote_ssh_config` → `ouroboros.config`, and `ouroboros.utils`, a declared bundle member that really travels, → `ouroboros.observability`, which does not). Both are fixed at the source: the operational SSH timeout table moved INTO `remote_ssh_config` (env-only integers that never needed Home; `config.get_ssh_timeout_sec` delegates to it so callers still read one table) and `sanitize_tool_args_for_log` moved OUT of `utils` into `observability`, beside the redactor it needs — every caller was Home-side anyway. The compensating smoke now exists for real in `tests/test_remote_native_isolation.py`: a fresh `-E` interpreter imports each kernel module and each closure seed behind a meta-path finder that refuses every forbidden prefix, which proves the import WORKS with no Home module on the path and is a runtime axis AST cannot give (its own limit, stated: it observes import time, so an uncalled function-local import is the static gate's job). A `try:`/`if:` wrapper is not an escape hatch because it still runs at import. (RWS v2 §3.1, root matrix) also the ROUTING tables: `REMOTE_NATIVE_TOOL_OPERATION` names the native operation an ssh dispatch runs per TOOL, and `ROOT_LABELLED_TOOL_ARG` + `dispatch_resource_root(tool, args)` answer the CALL half — which resource root THIS invocation resolves under, so the routing point can consult `workspace_ref.root_is_target_native`. The two are separate answers because per tool is structurally unable to decide placement: `read_file` is one tool serving both the target's worktree and Home's artifact store, so a per-tool answer necessarily sends both to the same host — and it sent both to the target, which does not model `root` and answered about its own workspace. A tool absent from `ROOT_LABELLED_TOOL_ARG` carries no root label as a POSITIVE statement rather than a gap (a process cwd, the workspace's own git, a workspace-relative media path are about the active workspace by construction), and `IMPLICIT_RESOURCE_ROOT` is spelled here too because routing reads the caller's RAW arguments, before any handler applies its own default
      ├── patch_core.py       ← Patch and batch-edit CORE shared by BOTH routes: envelope parsing, context-anchored hunk location, text application, and `plan_patch` — the resolve-everything-THEN-write order that IS `apply_patch`'s atomicity guarantee. Pure text: no filesystem, no policy, no context object, and deliberately no import of the tool registry, because the target's kernel could not load one. Each route supplies only its own doors (`resolve`/`exists`/`read_text`), so Home's binding-aware target resolver and the target's `native_mutation_target` are the ONLY difference between them. It exists as one module because a second copy of a patch planner would be free to drift on exactly that write order — and the divergence would be invisible until a patch applied half-way on one host and fully on the other
      ├── tool_access_policy.py ← The access VOCABULARY: the three axes (profile x resource root x operation) and the `_POLICY` matrix they index, plus `ResolvedResourceBinding` — the sealed ANSWER to one dispatch's question. Split out because everything in `tool_access` is WRITTEN in these terms and this depends on none of it; a vocabulary that imported its own users would make every question about access circular
      ├── tool_access.py       ← Tool API v2 policy matrix: ToolProfile × ResourceRoot × Operation; also projects the side-effect-free filesystem affordance map injected into runtime context and checks closed-enum subagent required_capabilities against the selected profile. `resolve_shell_cwd` is a PURE fact resolver (no mkdir — materialization lives in `workspace_executor.ensure_execution_cwd`); The operation's cwd is resolved ONCE per dispatch and every later consumer READS that answer rather than recomputing it. The carrier is `ResolvedResourceBinding`: `build_resolved_resource_binding` runs the resolver body (`_select_process_target`) a single time in `registry._dispatch` and the resulting value — logical root, base path, exact target path, source — is threaded to each guard as an argument. This branch first held the same invariant with an operation-scoped memo (`ShellCwdResolutions`); the sealed value is the stronger form and replaced it, because a memo makes re-resolution CHEAP while a value makes it IMPOSSIBLE, and a consumer holding the binding has no resolver call left to make. Error precedence is preserved for free: the one resolution either produces a binding or raises, at the one site, so no call site can meet a failure some other site already absorbed. Outside a dispatch (admission, CLI, tests) `resolve_shell_cwd` resolves directly and unbound. (RWS v2, root matrix) Under an ssh placement the resolver refuses TYPED (`RemoteWorkspacePathError`) before building any candidate, and `resource_root_path`/`resolve_resource_path` refuse `root=active_workspace` the same way while continuing to serve every Home-native root — a remote process has no Home cwd, and returning one would land a remote operation on the wrong filesystem
      ├── connection_store.py  ← (RWS v2, D6) THE owner-state store for remote SSH connection metadata (`data/state/remote_connections.json`): ONE module owns path/schema/lock/atomic-writer; records carry id, display name, ssh_alias (validated host token, never argv), pinned `expected_host_id` + full trust history, soft `active|retired` lifecycle — NO secrets (key material stays in the operator's own OpenSSH config). Persistence via the SSOT `utils.atomic_write_json`/`read_json_dict` + the `platform_layer` exclusive lock, both file and lock at 0o600. ALL readers/writers import this module — owner-only gateway handlers, the file/data-tool denial in `tools/core.py` (`is_connection_store_path` refuses the store plus its lock/tmp/hardlink aliases), the gateway file browser — the same owner-state discipline as settings/skill trust. Rationale (D6): the OS account is the DOCUMENTED trust boundary; only deterministic accidental-access guards exist, no shell command-blacklist theater

(D30) Also the account-surface translations — credential profiles, harness model discovery, setup/login jobs with their transient device-code snapshots — all read/translate only: the daemon owns every auth fact. Default discovery prefers the OWNED daemon once provisioned (`claudexor_daemon.owned_daemon_provisioned`), an explicit `home=` still reads that home verbatim, and `discover_daemon_at` reads the `CLAUDEXOR_CONFIG_DIR` override layout (`<config_dir>/daemon/control-api.json`)
      ├── claudexor_runtime.py ← Exact managed engine delivery. One reviewed pin selects the existing public Node-free Claudexor closure by version/build SHA/URL/SHA-256/size, protocol, exact tested Node, and entrypoint. New packages carry that compressed archive as a seed; an older app whose immutable resources predate the feature downloads the same bytes. Both paths verify size+digest, extract a regular-file tree into a private staging directory, run the side-effect-free identity probe with the host Node, and atomically promote one immutable version under the deliberately short `data/state/cx` root (separate from auth/config/runs, and short enough for the closure's package-manager paths on legacy Windows `MAX_PATH`). Directory names carry version plus a SHA prefix for path economy; the full build SHA, archive digest/size and probe result remain the admitted identity. There is no mutable `current` pointer or background updater: the code pin is the next-spawn selection. Status is read-only. A newer tree may be staged beside a live older daemon, which continues until its next natural start
      ├── claudexor_daemon.py  ← (D30) The Ouroboros-OWNED claudexord: data-plane config dir (`data/claudexor` as `CLAUDEXOR_CONFIG_DIR` — the override IS the complete relocatable root; the operator's `~/.claudexor` is never read or imported), supervision after the local-model template (`process_custody.spawn_supervised`, session scope, custody-reaped by fingerprint), ATTACH-IF-ALIVE (a live daemon on our home is attached, never duplicated), OWN-ONLY-IF-SELF-STARTED (`stop` terminates only a self-spawned process; a foreign daemon is never killed here). Provisioning is an OWNER ACTION (Connect or an actual delegated/reviewer start) — never a boot-time/status-read side effect. `ensure_owned_gateway` is the one explicit install/probe/spawn seam above the pure transport gateway: it stages the reviewed target in the foreground, preserves an already-live authenticated older daemon without hot swap, and selects the exact target on the next spawn. `OUROBOROS_CLAUDEXOR_BIN` remains an explicit operator override; a pinned managed-runtime failure never silently launches an arbitrary PATH binary. STALE LIFECYCLE: liveness is an AUTHENTICATED handshake (the per-home bearer token is the identity proof — only OUR daemon can accept it); a dead daemon whose home carries our ownership marker (`ouroboros-owned.json`, written at provision, naming our data plane) is restarted under the same supervision and RECONCILED by fresh discovery + authenticated handshake against the rewritten descriptor; a live responder that refuses the token is a FOREIGN daemon on a recycled port — typed `foreign_daemon` disclosure, never killed, never blocking the restart of our own dead daemon; a home whose marker names ANOTHER data plane is refused typed (`foreign_daemon_home`) before any spawn — restart there would be adoption

      │   ├── router.py        ← Starlette route collector for /api/* and /ws

      ├── task_placement.py ← WHERE a task runs: read the project's placement, fold in an explicit executor ref, preflight, SEAL it into the task metadata, and only then export attachments to wherever the answer said. The fixed order is the readable part; the seal is the load-bearing one — once written, a placement is not a suggestion the queue may re-derive
      │   ├── tasks.py         ← Headless task create/list/get/cancel/events endpoints over the supervisor queue. (RWS v2 §3.1) `_admit_task_placement` resolves the task's placement ONCE through the admission SSOT and `_seal_placement_into_metadata` writes it into `metadata[_sealed_workspace_ref]` — the single seal, which is why a project rebind after admission cannot move a running task (nothing re-resolves it). A REMOTE placement is INHERITED FROM THE PROJECT and can never be chosen per task: `workspace_ref`/`connection_id` in the body, or either key in `metadata`, is a typed 400 — ONE loop in `_admit_task_placement` checks both doors, because the body was checked and `metadata` was not: `metadata.workspace_ref` was silently STRIPPED by `_RESERVED_METADATA_KEYS` and `metadata.connection_id` was not in that set at all, so it was stored and then ignored, letting the owner name a placement nothing honoured. The seal's own private key (`_sealed_workspace_ref`) stays a strip rather than a refusal: it is not a spelling anyone chooses to express intent, it is what a RETURNED metadata blob contains, and a client replaying one must not become a 400. That is not ceremony — the project registry is where `routing_generation` and the connection's trust identity live, so a self-chosen remote target would be a placement with no generation to fence and no trust to revalidate. `_placement_display_root` is the placement's OWN spelling (Home path for local, target-native POSIX root for ssh) and is deliberately a string: it feeds the owner-visible plumbing while the sealed ref decides whether that spelling may ever become a `pathlib.Path`. `executor_ref` is refused for an ssh placement (it is a derived projection, §3.1) and a remote workspace's preflight is a disclosed-degraded summary rather than a Home walk over a tree that lives elsewhere. A task that inherits a REMOTE placement also carries the placement FENCE (`placement_fence_for`): this handler resolves the project's placement and only then enqueues, so the owner can rebind the project or retire the connection inside that window, and `supervisor/queue.py` refuses the stale insert under `_queue_lock` instead of aiming work at the previous target. The fence is set for a remote placement ONLY, so the local path keeps its exact refusal codes

A daemon that cannot answer is a typed 503 that persists nothing and keeps the wizard open

Zero auth logic on this side; the browser never sees the daemon token. The fallback login card is a copy-paste `claudexor setup attach …` command for the user's own terminal — no in-app terminal exists. The snapshot proxy is a VERBATIM pass-through, and that is load-bearing for the card: a `failed` job whose reason is a verification race is judged `recheck` (codex clears its auth store when a login STARTS, so a probe in that window lies), but when the bounded re-check runs out the card's verdict text is a fixed CONSTANT, so the engine's own `message` is the only thing that can say WHY. `harness_accounts.jobDetail()` reads it at both envelope levels and renders it beside a settled non-success verdict only — escaped, never beside "Connected." where a stale message would contradict the outcome, never while the job is pending (the live status line owns the card then), and never truncated (BIBLE P1: this is an owner-facing surface)
      │   ├── host_service.py  ← Loopback-only Host Service API for reviewed skill callbacks

      │   ├── connections.py   ← (RWS v2, D6) Owner-only `/api/owner/connections` HTTP surface: TRANSPORT-THIN handlers over `ouroboros/connection_store.py` (the store owns path/schema/lock/atomic writer — this module never re-implements persistence) plus the BOUNDED live projection of the remote workspace broker. Durable trust/lifecycle facts and live/health facts stay structurally separate: a broker status projection can never manufacture Home's own admission evidence — durable bootstrap compatibility (`connection_store.record_bootstrap`, so a restart does not un-bootstrap a host) plus process-local health freshness — because `api_connections_list` layers that evidence LAST, and trust is pinned ONLY by a successful bootstrap (an identity change ⇒ typed `host_identity_changed` + explicit owner retrust, never a silent re-pin). Live diagnostics are scrubbed and length/breadth-bounded before they reach a WS frame (`connection_state`), and every bound DISCLOSES itself: each capped list ships its pre-cap total (`log_refs_count`/`warnings_count` — one number, not a number plus a `*_truncated` flag that could disagree with it, since truncation IS `count > len(list)`), and a collection trimmed inside a diagnostic keeps a visible omission entry the way the depth guard already said `[truncated]`. A cap that leaves no trace reads exactly like data that was never there, which is the worst ambiguity to leave in the payload an operator opens specifically to find out what is missing — the CLI and both browser surfaces render the count rather than ending the list silently; destructive lifecycle ops (retrust/retire) fail CLOSED on any live-task/lease lookup failure and re-check under `supervisor/queue.py::_queue_lock`
      │   ├── projects.py      ← Multi-project CRUD surface (v6.32.0): GET /api/projects, POST /api/projects, POST /api/projects/from-task (bind an existing task to a new project). (v6.33.0 removed the /sleep + /wake status endpoints.) (RWS v2) `POST /api/projects` gained a FIFTH source — `connection_id` + `remote_root`, the two halves of a remote placement — and `POST /api/projects/{id}/update` gained the matching REBIND beside its rename. Both delegate to `workspace_admission.admit_remote_placement` and judge nothing themselves (a gateway that judged placements would be the second admission authority the v1 postmortem was about); they only map its typed refusals onto status codes (400 `invalid_remote_placement` for a malformed request or a path that is not the target's canonical worktree root, 503 `remote_transport_unavailable` when the target cannot be consulted at all, 409 `project_routing_generation_changed` for a rebind that lost the compare-and-set, 409 `project_has_live_tasks` for a rebind under running work). Admission OPENS a broker session on the target, so every path where that session did not become the project's placement runs `_close_provisional_project_session`, which re-reads the registry first because a concurrent request may have made this very ref authoritative. The live-task check FAILS CLOSED — three answers, not two, so "could not tell" refuses instead of reading as "no live tasks" (it returned `False` from a blanket `except`, which is the value that lets the rebind through, so any failure inside the lookup turned the guard into a green light; `gateway/connections._connection_busy` already answered this shape of question this way). A rebind is refused BEFORE admission when the project has live tasks: their placement was sealed at their own admission and nothing re-resolves it, so the rebind could not redirect them and the owner would believe the project had moved while work kept writing to the old host

      ├── tools/               ← Auto-discovered tool plugins
      │   ├── extension_dispatch.py ← Extension tool dispatch helper extracted from registry.py; preserves liveness, safety, async, and out-of-process error contracts
      │   ├── dispatch_policy.py ← (RWS v2) The PLACEMENT-BLIND home of policies that used to live inside Home handler BODIES, which is why they silently stopped applying the moment a task was pointed at a remote workspace ("one policy × N doors", with the second door being a placement rather than a channel): `subagent_secret_path_refusal` (the fail-closed restricted-subagent secret/control denial — a restricted subagent really did read `.env` and list `secrets/` out of a remote workspace), `script_interpreter_refusal`, and `filter_native_listing` (the listing filter the native route would otherwise skip). Called from the dispatch pipeline BEFORE the native/local fork, so both routes answer identically. It was described in the §3.1 prose and missing from this map, which is a P6 defect on its own: a module a reader cannot find from the map is a module the next reader re-invents
      │   ├── registry.py        ← Tool registry SSOT and dispatch pipeline: schemas, ToolContext, and the per-dispatch guard chain (path-arg normalization, ephemeral/subagent/heal/light gates, python pre-resolution, LLM safety, post-exec checks). The run_command shell-policy body lives in shell_guards.py; registry keeps the thin traced `_run_shell_safety_check` seam plus the registry-resident checks it injects (protected-path and git/runtime tails, the scope-floor detector with its read-only-inspection allowlist). (RWS v2 §3.1) `execute` is a thin boundary — it opens the per-dispatch `OutstandingPrepare` register, hands off to `_dispatch`, classifies an unclaimed `RemoteWorkspacePathError`, and withdraws in a `finally` — while the whole guard pipeline lives in `_dispatch`. TWO facts are resolved exactly once there and threaded onward: the PLACEMENT, by `prepare_operation` (the pipeline's single placement-ROUTING site, pinned by a TRANSITIVE AST test that walks the pipeline from `execute` over every function defined in this module — the earlier scan read `execute` and `_dispatch` alone, so extracting the gate chain took its pre-prepare reads out of view in the same commit that introduced them; every reachable read is now enumerated with its reason in `_DECLARED_PLACEMENT_READS`), and the HOME cwd, by `_home_resolved_binding`. That gate asks whether the operation ADDRESSES Home — `not _prepared.native_routed` — and not whether the task is remote. Keying it on the placement was a real defect: by the root matrix a remote task calling a HOME-native root keeps its Home handler, so skipping the binding sent every guard to its `None` fallback, which reads `workspace_mode`, true for any admitted remote task — and `runtime_mode=light` stopped protecting Home's own repo and data drive for every ssh task. It reads `_prepared`, not the sealed placement, so it is not a second door onto it; a TARGET-ROUTED operation gets no binding, because a Home path is not a fact it has. Rationale for resolving up front: without it the cwd is first computed by whichever guard happens to look first — the resolution site becomes "wherever policy looks", which is the desync shape a guard/executor split has to make impossible. (RWS-05) `_root_relative_normalizer` picks the normalizer for a path arg in THAT ROOT'S NATIVE SPELLING SPACE: an ssh `active_workspace` normalizes against the sealed ref's target root with pure posix semantics, every other root and every local/docker placement keep the Home resolver byte-for-byte, and `root=user_files` never auto-routes under an ssh placement because a Home absolute path cannot be "under" a remote workspace (comparing the two spellings at all is the bug). `active_repo_dir_for` refuses TYPED for an ssh placement instead of falling through to `ctx.repo_dir` — that fallback would silently aim a remote task's active workspace at the live Ouroboros repo; `system_repo_dir_for` is Home-native under every placement. `ToolContext.is_workspace_mode()` reads the SEALED placement rather than the Home path: an ssh placement has no `workspace_root`, and answering False for it would put a remote task on the workspace-less `self_modification` profile over the live repo — so the ref answers, and `workspace_mode_block_reason` (which guards overlap with HOME roots, structurally impossible for a target-native root) stays local-only. `ToolContext.active_repo_dir()` keeps its Home-local contract and refuses typed for ssh at the same seam. (RWS v2 §3.1 step 4) `_dispatch` ends in a fork: a NATIVE route hands the authorized token to `dispatch_execute.execute_native_operation` and returns its text, while every local/docker/Home-local dispatch continues into `_invoke_builtin_handler` byte-identically.
